#6801. Angelina Jolie Masters Timeless Autumnal Layering at Dinner With Sons Pax and Knox

The leaves are changing, the temperatures are cooling, and it’s finally time for wool coats. After weeks of feeling like summer would never let up, we’ve officially transitioned into the cozy season—just ahead of everyone’s favorite autumnal holiday. And last night, some two weeks ahead of Thanksgiving, Angelina Jolie got the family time started.

The actor was spotted grabbing dinner with sons Pax and Knox at West Hollywood hotspot Cecconi’s. She looked effortlessly refined for the outing, bundling up in layers that included a loose white button-down shirt and a brown wool coat.

Her wide-leg gray trousers, designed with a slouchy, relaxed silhouette, reached all the way to the ground, covering her black pointed-toe footwear.

Jolie, who shares Pax, Knox, and their four siblings with ex-husband Brad Pitt, wore the classic, comfy ensemble with black sunglasses and an ornate silver pendant necklace crafted with delicate filigree work.

Next to her, Pax—aged 22—wore a varsity-inspired navy-blue jacket over a white T-shirt and dark jeans. His black-and-white sneakers appeared to be a pair of low tops from xVessel’s collaboration with Yohji Yamamoto, recognizable for its chunky, deconstructed sole.

Knox, on the other hand, went for a lighter look with a white T-shirt from Shihiko, embroidered with the phrase, “It’s been a long day.” The experimental piece, worn here without a coat, was printed with a hidden UV reactive graphic of a cat, which only appeared under sunlight. The 18-year-old tucked the tee into brown trousers and finished things off with a pop of yellow, by way of his smiley cap-toe Prospective Flow sneakers.

#6799. I Tried Taylor Swift’s Posture-Correcting Sports Bra—and I Have Some Thoughts

Is the $218 price tag worth it? Here’s the verdict.

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I have clocked a handful of years as a shopping editor, which means that my eye is trained to see things other people might miss. For example, when we were all oohing and ahhing over Taylor Swift’s TIME Person of the Year profile, I noticed that TayTay was wearing an interesting sports bra in one of her Eras Tour dance rehearsal photos. I did some digging, and it turns out that she was rocking the Forme Power Bra, a posture-correcting bra designed to create proper alignment in your shoulders and upper back. Fancy, fancy!

Considering how grueling Taylor’s Eras Tour rehearsals were (“Every day I would run on the treadmill, singing the entire set list out loud,” she told TIME), having supportive gear designed by an orthopedic surgeon makes all the sense in the world.

And while my days consist more of imagining I’m performing “Cruel Summer” while Hot Girl Walking on the treadmill than actually doing stadium tours, I immediately thought to myself that the Forme Power Bra might just be the posture solution I needed. Like, why yes, I’d love to sit less like a shrimp and more like a 5’11” pop star engaged to an NFL player. So I, along with a couple of fellow Cosmo editors, tested it out—because if I can't have an eight-carat diamond engagement ring (or a wedding at Madison Square Garden), I'll take this instead.

Keep reading to get the real scoop on whether Taylor’s posture-correcting sports bra is worth the $$$—and which version to snag—below.

If the phrase “posture-correcting device” conjures up images of a scary, hardware-filled contraption, think again. The sports bra is less brace-like and more supportive, using fabrics of various tensions throughout the design to keep your shoulders back and gently guide your body into correct alignment whether you’re standing up, sitting down, or exercising.

The Power Bra comes in a range of colors in sizes XS to 3XL. I’ll admit it’s not the most affordable thing out there at $188. But it’s definitely fancier than your average sports bra thanks to all the FDA-registered technology—you can even spend your pre-tax HSA money on it if you want!

When I first reviewed this bra a couple of years ago, my biggest wish was that it had some sort of inner shelf bra lining or padding. And in 2025, the brand fixed that!

Forme debuted an upgraded Power+ Bra last year, a premium style that features discreet, removable padding and a wider underarm opening to allow for more mobility. It now comes in seven colors and has a slightly higher price tag at $218.

For someone like me who prefers a little nip coverage, it's totally worth opting for this slightly-upgraded option. I know myself, and I'm just way more likely to wear this one to the gym or under clothes because it functions more like a sports bra or regular bra thanks to the padding.

To illustrate my commitment to testing all the things that Blondie owns, I will tell you all that I did, in fact, try on the sports bra in a very tiny office bathroom stall (pls see photo evidence below). And not going to lie, it was a little tricky to put on at first. But don't let that scare you! It was easier to put on the second time, and even easier the third time, and the fourth time, and so on. The brand recommends stretching the armhole a few times before wearing it, which I found to be helpful.

My initial reaction was that the Forme bra provided instant relief from my slouch-induced shoulder and neck pain. It really does help you be more mindful of your posture. I even tried to curve my shoulders forward at one point, and wildly enough, realized it was way harder than normal to slouch my shoulders into Gremlin Mode. It acted as a gentle reminder that the most comfortable way to sit is with my shoulders down and back.

Also, it’s worth noting that I have a mild case of scoliosis, and I was worried that the bra would feel like a brace because the natural resting state of my spine is a liiiiittle bit off center. But I didn’t run into any issues—it even supported my one shoulder that has a tendency to pop out!

As far as sizing, Cosmo’s Deputy Shopping Director Kim Duong and I tried on our regular sizes, and we both found the sports bra to be pretty dang tight. It’s very compressive and lacks cups—this led to flat-from-the-side, noticeable-from-the-front Regency-style cleave à la Bridgerton, so I’d suggest sizing up if you have a large chest. Kim and I agreed at the time that the Power Bra, which is made of a pretty thin, nippy athletic material, would be vastly improved with the addition of removable cups or inserts—and in August 2025, the brand heard our prayers with the Power+ Bra that features removable padding!

Beauty Editor Beth Gillette also tested it out: "I was hesitant—I'd read reviews from our team but hadn't seen anyone in my size (14, DD chest) rave about it. But I might actually love it more than Kim and Hannah! It's very comfortable, shockingly, considering I have horrendous posture, according to my chiropractor. It pulls back my shoulders a bit without pinching anything and doesn't feel unnatural, either," Gillette raved.

"And I love how good it looks," she continued. "I am not particularly ~gifted~ in the ~perky~ boob department. I often describe my boobs as living permanently in my armpits. But! This lifts and shapes them rather nicely. I felt v confident walking around in it and even felt compelled to take some selfies, which is how you know."

I enjoyed the Power Bra the most while wearing it on my busiest, most active days. I’m talkin’ workout classes, running to catch the bus, and hauling boxes out of your friend’s apartment—on those days, the garment felt less tight and more supportive in the same way that a good pair of compression leggings or a high-impact sports bra helps with your performance.

However, I struggled to wear it for long periods of time on work days spent typing away at my desk, especially when I first started wearing the Power Bra. I was always dyingggg to take the sports bra off by the end of the day. My body would be tired of being reminded to sit perfectly straight for hours, and it felt great to rip off the posture-correcting garment.

But after getting used to the sports bra over the course of more than a year, I actually feel relief putting the Power Bra on after a day of slouching. It's a way of reminding my body that it ultimately feels the best when it's aligned correctly rather than automatically slumping into a weird, compressed curve. But it's definitely something that takes practiced stamina to wear for long periods of time, so don't give up!

Kim had a similar experience: “It’s not the miracle bra one may hope it is. It definitely requires some active willpower to transform your slouching habits,” she explained. “Overall, I think it would be a good bra to wear while you’re running errands or rehearsing for a worldwide tour, but I found it to unfortunately be too uncomfy for me to wear at my desk.”

Beth, on the other hand, totally fell in love with the bra—especially for workouts. "My Pilates instructor actually kept complimenting my form(e, hehe), and even kept nudging me to add heavier resistance and to do more intense poses," she noted. "In her words, I was 'a pro." And I'll be totally honest, no instructor has ever, ever, ever said that to me, so I have to believe that some of it is because of this bra."

And as far as results? While wearing the Power Bra would give me an instant posture boost, I honestly haven't seen a noticeable, lasting difference in my posture despite “training” it by wearing the Power Bra under my clothes. When I took it off, I felt like I sagged right back into my slight but regular slouch. All of the posture magic seems to happen while you’re wearing the Power Bra, which is why I’d recommend it as extra support during a yoga class, dance rehearsals, or a super active day… not as something to wear Every! Single! Day! in hopes of magically curing your posture.

Taylor's sports bra comes in a bunch of colors...

While the Power Bra didn't transform me into a billionaire with an NFL husband (🥲), I totally understand why Taylor invested in this product for her dance rehearsals. The Forme bra (with stretchier fabric in the front and tighter fabric in the back to align your shoulders properly!) is a great way to remember to mind your posture when you’re being active, especially for shorter periods of time when you can tolerate the tight compression of the fabric.

And although I haven't seen any mind-blowing, permanent results over more than a year of testing, anything that reminds me what correct alignment should feel like (for both aesthetic and general health purposes) feels like a win in the long run. It was super easy to wear under clothes, so while it’s probably not going to be an everyday bra for me, I’ll definitely be taking advantage of it for Pilates classes, hiking, or more physically taxing days.

Next step? Learning Taylor’s Vigilante Sh*t choreo...

She previously wrote for Seventeen and CR Fashion Book. Follow her on Instagram for hot takes on red carpet fashion and pictures of her office outfits that nobody asked for.

#6794. Can Men Have Multiple Orgasms?

One of the many reasons I love having a vagina is because the vagina famously has no refractory period—meaning that once I orgasm, I don’t technically need a break after one orgasm to experience another (unless I’m feeling super sensitive). In fact, I can have multiple Os at once. Iconic.

But I’m just finding out that people with penises might be able to do the same thing.

Yes, the classic male orgasm comes with an intermission, but some men can have two, three, or even more in a single sexual encounter. And no, we’re not talking about round two after a 20-minute break. Some men report orgasming again within seconds or minutes, sometimes without ejaculating at all.

So what’s going on physiologically? And can men actually learn to do this?

To understand how multiples are possible, it helps to know what happens during a typical male orgasm. “At climax, the brain processes intense pleasure while the nervous system coordinates involuntary, rhythmic contractions of the pelvic floor muscles,” explains board-certified urologist Dr. David Shusterman. Heart rate and breathing climb, muscle tension peaks, and then the body relaxes. If ejaculation accompanies the orgasm, semen moves into the urethra and is propelled out by muscular contractions.

Then comes the refractory period I mentioned earlier, a recovery phase in which another erection or orgasm is difficult or temporarily impossible. It can last anywhere from minutes to hours, or even longer. That’s the main reason multiples are harder for men. Male and female orgasms both involve the brain, sensory nerves, and rhythmic pelvic contractions, Dr. Shusterman says. The difference is what happens afterward.

That’s also where orgasm and ejaculation come in. They usually happen together, but they’re separate processes: orgasm is the pleasurable sensation of climax, and ejaculation is the physical release of semen. One can happen without the other, which may be why some men who report multiples skip the recovery phase altogether. Without ejaculation, they may stay sexually responsive.

Considering how much attention other aspects of men’s sexual health get, there’s surprisingly little research on this phenomenon. A 2016 review in Sexual Medicine Reviews, “Multiple Orgasms in Men—What We Know So Far,” estimated that fewer than 10 percent of men in their 20s and fewer than 7 percent of men over 30 are capable of them. The researchers stressed that the evidence was limited. They also identified two patterns. Sporadic multiple orgasms involve one orgasm, a wait of several minutes, and then another. Condensed multiple orgasms are a burst of two to four within seconds or a couple of minutes. Either way, they don’t always involve multiple ejaculations.

In a 1989 study in Archives of Sexual Behavior, researchers interviewed 21 men who reported multiple orgasms. Some had orgasms without ejaculation, both before and after an ejaculatory one, and some had a series of orgasms without losing their erections. More recently, a 2021 survey in The Journal of Sexual Medicine of 122 men found that people reported anywhere from two to 30 orgasms in a single encounter, though most said two to four. Most also kept their erections throughout and ejaculated with every orgasm.

A 2025 qualitative study in Sexual and Relationship Therapy interviewed 12 men who said they were capable of multiples. Ten said they could have them without ejaculating. Their techniques included mental and breathing control, pelvic floor muscle control, sex toys, and abstaining from sex beforehand. Some said they’d always been able to, while others learned over time.

Still, this was a very small study based on participants’ own accounts, not a controlled experiment. These techniques may be worth exploring, but there’s no guarantee they’ll turn anyone into a multiorgasmic man.

And if you’re not one, don’t panic. “Sexual satisfaction is not an orgasm count,” Dr. Shusterman says. “Having one orgasm, needing a longer recovery period, or not experiencing multiple orgasms does not by itself indicate a problem.”

Multiple orgasms might sound like the ultimate sexual superpower, but they’re hardly a requirement for a great sex life. Whether you’re having one, several, or none at all, the goal is to enjoy yourself.

#6793. Hanifa Is Relaunching After an 8-Month Hiatus

Hanifa is back. A little over eight months after founder Anifa Mvuemba paused operations for her label, which had been in business for 15 years, it’s relaunching with a new collection and updated business model.

The collection features the colorful party clothes the brand is known for, like a red evening gown with a bright blue feathered brooch and a blue liquid organza dress (a 2022 revival bound to become the season’s made-to-order showstopper). There are also more practical pieces, like Hanifa’s take on the track pant (barrel-legged with a silk stripe) and bomber jacket. The Harper dress, a green and black-dotted hit from last year, is now available in a deep blue.

Mvuemba hopes the new Hanifa feels true to the brand’s original ethos, just a little more mature: the designer was 21 when she started the brand in 2011, and now that she’s about to turn 36, her style and taste has evolved, she says, alongside her customers’. Mvuemba spent months reworking the brand’s fabrications, cuts and patterns, especially for knitwear, its biggest category. She went through dozens of iterations to find the right cashmere blend, for example, that will hold its shape and have cashmere’s soft handfeel, while keeping the brand in the price range its customers have come to expect.

The brand worked to improve its fabric quality without compromising on price.

The relaunch will be a test in regaining consumer trust. Things began to unwind last December, when purchases made during her brand’s annual Hanifa Friday sale in November failed to ship, thanks to production delays and supply chain issues. For some customers, they said months went by without any communication — or delivery — from the brand. Disgruntled customers took to social media to complain, not just about delayed orders (all of which Hanifa said it was eventually able to fulfill, or issued refunds for), but about the brand’s general direction, which they believed went downhill. In a statement posted online in March, Mvuemba apologized, and announced the brand would go on hiatus.

“Mentally, I wasn’t prepared to continue,” Mvuemba says. “I wasn’t in the right headspace. And in order for me to be the best version of myself, the best leader, mother, wife, I could be to my family, my team, my business — I needed that mental clarity to just take a step back, rest, recalibrate, to get a clear view on what we needed to do for the business as a whole. I needed a break,” Mvuemba says. “I’m just not the type of founder or person that performs for the optics. It was extremely hard to do, but it was necessary, because in this space, it’s not something that we see often. But it’s something that we as founders need to be able to do.”

Hanifa’s new collection comes after the brand paused orders in March.

In the end, Mvuemba only took about two weeks off from work, she says. She found a therapist and spent time with her baby, who was born in December and whose maternity care was interrupted by crisis management in the fallout. Then, she turned her attention to Hanifa 2.0.

“I love this business so much. I love this brand so much. I had to get back,” she says. “We uprooted everything. We looked at our communications, our branding, our sizing, our website. Now, everything — our processes, our manufacturers — has shifted, and it’s nice to have had this time to sit through everything, go back, revise, go back, revise, and then say, ‘OK, we’re ready.’”

Now the brand is ready to relaunch, Hanifa is returning to market on different terms.

Mvuemba is eyeing outerwear as an expansion opportunity for her collection.

The biggest change to Hanifa’s operations is switching to a fully ready-to-ship model, versus a past pre-order model. With no pre-order period, where the brand could produce only a committed number of SKUs, the company has fronted the entire new collection (which Mvuemba says is “expensive but necessary”) and plans to use real-time performance data to determine production cadence and inventory investments. Having inventory overhead also shortens lead times for purchases to be fulfilled, and gives the brand space to ship items by sea rather than air, which cuts costs.

“We thought it was really important to come out the door swinging,” Mvuemba says. “People want their things when they want their things.” It’s also invested in new customer service and logistics partners, with brand plans to build data analysis into its production cycle to ensure that it’s responding quicker to what’s selling. Mvuemba hired a brand strategist, an art director and a PR firm to help steer the comeback story. The brand has always been bootstrapped, but she says she’s open to outside funding if the right partner comes along. She’s also open to the right retail partners, but that right now, the direct-to-consumer model is the best fit thanks to production and shipping timing.

Her focus above all, she says, is her customer. Mvuemba comes back time and again to the importance of data, and how much she wants to spend the rollout of this collection listening to feedback. She hopes that the brand’s core customer — the company has a legion of longtime loyalists who were sad to see it go — returns, and that the brand can grow its reach. By category, she’s excited about expanding the brand’s shoes and outerwear, both of which are steps in building a more complete ready-to-wear label, which is her main goal for next year. Scaling, Mvuemba says, will come at the right pace.

“I used to say I want it to be as big as LVMH. And with this year, I just had so much time to reflect and I’m OK with the pace that we’re going,” she says. “I just want to have a globally recognized brand that a lot of women can identify with and that we can continue to create these beautiful, amazing pieces. I’m so excited to show them this new era of the brand. We’re sharper, we’re clearer, we understand who our girl is, who our customer is, and I’m excited to give them more.”

#6789. Amanda Seyfried Lights Up Tiffany & Co.’s Iconic Bird on a Rock to Usher in the Holiday Season

On Tuesday evening, all that glitters was indeed gold—and adorned in diamonds. For the third year in a row, Tiffany & Co.’s festive fête at its Fifth Avenue flagship served as the unofficial ribbon-cutting moment to kick off the holiday season, hosted this time around by Amanda Seyfried.

The actress, who is the House’s newest ambassador, was also tapped to illuminate the Bird on a Rock installation. A glitzed-up and glammed-out guest list made its way to Midtown for the occasion, passing the Fifth Avenue snowflake—now lit in Tiffany Blue for the holiday season—and stopping to marvel at the Landmark’s twinkling facade.

A brief elevator ride upstairs opened up to reveal a winter wonderland. Sparkling displays showcased the House’s pieces in front of the floor-to-ceiling windows, trays were laden with Tiffany Blue martinis, and the belle of the ball—the Bird on a Rock—gleamed outside. As guests counted down the moments until its lighting, they nibbled on petite T&C stamped-paninis and micro tuna crispy rice bites.

Seyfried arrived, dazzling in a Tiffany HardWear white gold and pavé diamond link necklace and the hallmark Bird on a Rock Wing brooch—with over 13 carats of diamonds. The award-winning star was just as excited for the party as everyone else. “As a young American girl, you have these fantasies that you might get to be a part of something really special. It’s pretty iconic that I’m here and that I get to represent Tiffany for the holiday season. There’s something very classic, traditional, and symbolic about Tiffany’s—it’s the best legacy jewelry brand there is,” Seyfried told Vogue. “My sister-in-law is here tonight with her baby, my godson. I wish my kids were here, but I didn’t even think to invite them, and they’re actually in the city. I don’t know what I was thinking!”

The holidays evoke all types of fuzzy feelings, and Mikey Madison reminisced about one of her favorite family traditions. “My dad and I always cook, so we’ll cook a big Hanukkah dinner and we’ll do all the classics that my grandma used to make. He does the brisket, I’ll do the latkes, the matzah ball soup, the kugel,” Madison shared. “It’s always meaningful to go to an event with Tiffany. It feels like a big family for me, and there’s so much intention behind their artistry, what they do, and how they bring people together,” the Oscar-winning ambassador added. “It’s the loveliest job that I have to get to work with them and get to wear all this incredibly beautiful jewelry as well.”

When it was finally time to light the Bird on a Rock, everyone gathered near the tree—one constructed from countless coveted Blue Boxes. “Love is a gift,” Matthieu Garnier, president and CEO of Tiffany & Co. North America, told the crowd during his toast. “The most meaningful gifts carry stories, create memories, and connect generations.”

Garnier wasn’t just referring to the tree: The entire 2026 holiday campaign—starring Seyfried—centers on gifts becoming future heirlooms, which in turn bridge a connection between generations. Just like in 1965, when Jean Schlumberger’s Bird on a Rock design became one of those treasured keepsakes intended to be passed down.

Right on cue, Seyfried stepped up, placing a Tiffany ornament on the tree, and immediately, the Bird on a Rock shimmered to life, illuminating the bustling streets below. Let the holidays begin!

Mikey Madison, Daniel Ramirez, Amanda Seyfried

#6786. GQ’s 13 Breakout Stars of 2026

Sometimes it’s fun to be early to the party. GQ launched the Breakouts portfolio in 2014 to celebrate the Hollywood upstarts who were poised to do big things. And not to brag, but we nailed it; that first group included Miles Teller, Shailene Woodley, Eddie Redmayne, and the late, great Chadwick Boseman. Over the ensuing years, we featured plenty more soon-to-be-superstars: Rami Malek. Brie Larson. Daniel Kaluuya. Riz Ahmed. Barry Keoghan. The kids from Stranger Things, when they were still kids. The cast of Succession before Succession became everyone’s favorite show.

In 2017, we put a baby-faced Timothée Chalamet on the list for his turn in Call Me by Your Name. (Our global entertainment director, Dana Mathews, fondly recalls him turning to her during the Man of the Year Party and saying, “This is the best party I’ve ever been to in my life.”) Since then, Chalamet has appeared on three GQ covers, earned four Oscar nominations, and become a household name (and maybe been to a few more great parties).

We took a few years off (okay, fine, seven), but 2026 feels like exactly the right time to revive GQ’s Breakouts portfolio—now through a wider lens. Yes, though we’ve got eyes on Hollywood’s next big things—like, say, Sly Stallone doppelgänger Anthony Ippolito; Gen Z class clown Owen Thiele, of Adults; Misty Green’s astounding leading woman, Rosalind Eleazar—not everyone is working the camera. We’re excited to introduce you to the West Coast’s hottest up-and-coming rapper in AZ Chike and put you on to Fcukers, whose debut album, packed with rowdy electro-pop bangers, made them first in our hearts and on our pregaming playlists. We’ve also got podcaster and journalist Pablo Torre, whose relentless reporting upended a $7.8 billion NBA franchise, and Jordan Firstman, an internet-born comedian who wrote and directed a film that commanded a lengthy standing ovation from Cannes audiences (right after they finished crying).

Point is, our Breakouts class of 2026 brings together 13 fully minted stars who did something that feels increasingly impossible: They captured our attention in a time when it feels like attention is more fleeting than ever. Welcome to their party.

Dress by Dior. Shoes by Jimmy Choo. Mask from Palace Costume Co.

Age: 25Breakout moment: Playing the queen bee on Ryan Murphy’s ’80s glam fever dream The Shards.—————

It’s a different kind of attention that I actually prefer. Because for so long I was getting attention, but not for a specific thing. And that was confusing and kind of messed with my head a bit. I was really young and people knew who I was, but I couldn’t really point to the work that they knew. And so it’s really fun for me to have people [now say], “I watched The Shards,” or “I watched Bottoms,” and then we have something to talk about.

Infinite Jest—no, I’m kidding, they’ve already read it, all of them. A Man’s Place by Annie Ernaux is very good and easily digestible. I think Just Kids is wonderful. What We Talk About When We Talk About Love. That one’s really great. Oh, and this is so classic, but Nine Stories by J.D. Salinger is really good. I know guys—male actors especially—love The Catcher in the Rye.

I got really worried the other day because I started getting a lot of followers. It was two days of so many followers, and I was like, “Oh my God, I’ve been doxxed.” The last thing I logged was Hitch. My top four are A Woman Under the Influence, The Dark Knight, Phantom Thread, and Eternal Sunshine. My Odyssey review has 112 likes. I don’t care how many followers I have on Instagram, how many likes I get. Letterboxd is truly a hit of adrenaline every time someone likes my review. —Frazier Tharpe

Jacket and jeans by Ralph Lauren Collection. Necklace by Chopard. Ring by Chanel Fine Jewelry.

Age: 28Breakout Moment: The series Off Campus, as the hottest fictional hockey player from this year’s hottest fictional hockey show.—————

I cannot shut up about this, but it has to be throwing the first pitch out for the White Sox. I will remember that forever.

Yeah, it was. And I hadn’t thrown a baseball in a really long time.

I look forward to the moment where I get to really reflect and digest. Next month I have some time off, and I’m going to go home and eat sandwiches at my mom’s house and take my grandma for a walk. “Go on a walk with my grandma”.… That’s a weird way to put it.

I’m, like, an idiot. I’m a full goofball. Garrett Graham is the heartthrob. To me, I’m just like, “I’m Bel.” And in my head, those are very different things. I’m flattered that people can associate me with heartthrob status, but no, I don’t wake up in the morning feeling like a heartthrob.

I mean, I always wanted to play Holden Caulfield, but who knows? —Trishna Rikhy

Age: 30Breakout Moment: Being the infectiously charismatic friend-to-all on FX’s zoomer sitcom Adults.—————

Sadie Sink knew who I was, and that was kind of cool. I just love her. She’s so talented and so adorable. It’s like meeting the queen before she’s the queen.

I do watch the fan edits. They’re fun. Some of them pick the worst photos of me, and I’m like, “This is a hate crime, but thank you for putting it on TikTok.”

I got two very smart pieces of advice. One was, “Run. Don’t do this. Save yourself.” And then the other person said, “Stick with it.” I’m kind of doing both. I am simultaneously sticking with it and trying my hardest, but also I’m like, Once you have a win, you do have to celebrate it and run as far away as you can and hide.

I bought a bag from The Row…which looks like—and I say this with love—it could be $20, but it’s the most expensive thing I own. And I did this because I went shopping with Hailey Bieber, and when you’re shopping with Hailey Bieber you buy things you think you can afford but you probably can’t…and now it’s too late to return. It’s a beautiful bag—it’s quiet luxury, babe. It’s so quiet you don’t know it’s The Row. —F.T

Age: 38Breakout moment: Her riveting performance as the title character in Chris Rock’s Misty Green (and her role in Slow Horses).—————

I mean, the first time I watched it, I just thought, Oh—you’re going to be in every frame. [Laughs]

[In an American accent] “Murderer.” Mur-der-rer. I think every Brit finds that hard. I was like, “Can I say ’killer’ instead?” And “mirror.” It’s when you’ve got a double r. But I had a great dialect coach.

I would’ve loved to have been in a scene with Philip Seymour Hoffman. That would’ve been my dream. And I would love to work with Sandra Hüller.

I was onstage with Ralph Fiennes. We finished Misty Green—I did an overnight shoot on Friday into Saturday morning, got on a plane, arrived in London on Sunday, and went straight into rehearsals with Ralph. It was a pretty mad summer.

I was getting ready before the Misty Green premiere [at the Toronto International Film Festival]. And my mom came down. She sat there and she was just sending pictures and she just had tears in her eyes. Just seeing this whole period at TIFF—for her, it’s her little girl. And every now and again, I would just look at her and she was just happy and in awe in a way. It warmed my heart. —A.P.

Age: 35Breakout moment: The Cannes hit Club Kid, which he wrote, directed, and starred in as a party promoter navigating unexpected parenthood.—————

I still do, yeah, at the phone call scene at the end. But it feels very distant from me. I feel like I’m watching it more as an observer now, which is nice, and I feel different than when I made it. I was such an open wound.

Cannes was such an emotional experience. I’m 35, and I’ve always known that I had something to say and I knew how to say it, but I’ve never been given the opportunity. So, honestly, to feel like I was being seen and understood was like 20 years of validation that I didn’t get. And so something shifted, that week, in me. I’ve noticed myself not having to use humor as a shield anymore, as much. I’m less afraid to be vulnerable—and not just in my public life but in my personal life too.

Of course, you shouldn’t need the validation of anyone to feel like you’re worthy, but let’s be real.…

What gagged me this year? I would say, honestly, as a statement of ambition, the Javis’ movie [La Bola Negra]. You marvel at it because you’re like, “How the fuck did they pull this off?” To even want to tell that complex of a story, and to just do it and go balls to the wall, is very inspiring.

I was like, “This is too long. It’s embarrassing now.” —A.P.

Jacket and pants by Canali. Shirt by Amiri. Boots by Alessandro Vasini. Tie by Husbands.

Age: 41Breakout moment: Reporting on how the Steve Ballmer–owned Clippers were allegedly funny with money, on his podcast Pablo Torre Finds Out, leading to one of the most severe team penalties in sports history.—————

Two days ago, I walked into this event that The New York Times was holding…it was like the 175th birthday party [for the paper]. I walk out the elevator and literally the first person I see is [NBA commissioner] Adam Silver. I won’t say exactly what he told me, but the tenor of our conversation was like, This is the HBO series adaptation of what this moment would be.

As a first-generation American—my parents are from the Philippines—there’s always been a dream of mine to be cast as a white person. So…Andrew Garfield, come on. Let’s do this.

The funny part is that I’m not even done reporting this story yet. The federal government is now investigating the Clippers, and so I’m now investigating the government’s investigation in the era of Trump too, which is like its own rabbit hole inside of a rabbit hole, which is fucking wild. But at the same time, it feels like now our number is listed in the phone book…actual other NBA owners are calling me up saying, “Can you look into this?”

It’s hard not to be sappy about this, but the lesson I learned is that if you’re telling the truth, you can walk into any room and feel like that as a suit of armor. Even when the person you’re investigating is one of the 10 richest people in the world. —F.T.

Coat by Michael Kors. Vintage pajamas from Palace Costume Co. Socks by London Sock Company. Glasses by Jacques Marie Mage. Necklaces (throughout), his own.

Age: 37Breakout moments: Stealing scenes as a ’70s Provincetown line cook in the Anthony Bourdain biopic Tony, and his stand-up special Uncle Stav.—————

I just did The Drew Barrymore Show. That experience was so magical. I was so nervous to be around Drew Barrymore. I’ve done a lot of cool shit, and that’s the one where I was like, “I am actually nervous to the point where I’m not a professional. I’m just a stammering little boy who’s with the girl he had a crush on from The Wedding Singer when he was a fat 10-year-old, and I’m trying to keep it together.”

Last week I allowed myself an aimlessly wander around New York City day. Got a little stoned, walked around, stumbled into a movie. Walked along the water on the West Side. Sat down to a little random omakase. Rode a Citi Bike around. I call it the hero’s journey, where you leave your home at, whatever, 11 a.m. ,with just the bare provisions, and you’re not allowed to go home. You just have to keep moving and keep doing stuff.

I filmed this Judd Apatow movie The Comeback King, with Glen Powell, Cristin Milioti, Madelyn Cline. That comes out in February. I’m excited to do stand-up—for fun, not to explicitly work on another hour. I’m excited to go back to clubs and just like, “Can I be the funniest person in this club tonight?” And that’s my only goal.

To be honest with you, the last three years, it’s [been] all go. But you got to have these big years, or else GQ doesn’t talk to you at the end of the year. —A.P.

In the thick of prep. I was training at Gleason’s Gym with Stephan James, who plays Carl Weathers. We were beating the crap out of each other and figuring out how to box. I had gained a bunch of weight, and I was trying to actually cut a few pounds, so I was in those sweatsuits, like a garbage-bag type thing. On the way to the gym, I would listen to Stallone and on the way home.… Everything I could find up to 1980, 1985, every piece of Stallone footage that existed.

Philip Seymour Hoffman is one. Chadwick Boseman. Christian Bale, Heath Ledger.

Ages: Ambrossi, 42 | Calvo, 35Breakout moment: Cowriting and directing La Bola Negra, the gay Spanish historical epic loosely based on an unfinished Federico García Lorca play.——————————————

Javier Ambrossi: Very important. We understood that sometimes people expect LGBTQ+ stories to be niche or for a specific audience. We wanted to do a queer story about universal love.

Ambrossi: It’s a way to put in context how different we are. We change a lot, and the story of our grandparents and our story is very different.

Javier Calvo: What do we do with the freedom our ancestors fought for, and died for?

Calvo: Isabelle Huppert. For me, she’s everything.

Ambrossi: I will go with Julia Roberts. My Best Friend’s Wedding is a very important queer story. The relationship between a woman and a gay man—it was the first time I saw that onscreen. And Rupert Everett was so bold, and I could tell in his eyes that he was gay. I was a kid and I was like, “He’s not faking it.”

How did you celebrate when you won best director at Cannes?

Ambrossi: I put the award on the table and went to sleep. And then Javi came over at 8 a.m.…

Ambrossi: “Give me the award!” And he took it! —A.P.

#6785. ‘DWTS’ Fans Are Side-Eyeing Tyler Cameron After He Reposted Jokes About Harry Shum Jr.’s Emotional Salsa Routine

Harry Shum Jr. and Jenna Johnson’s salsa routine unexpectedly brought tears to the Dancing With the Stars ballroom (and it had nothing to do with Jenna’s broken nose).

Before he and Jenna hit their steps to Lady Gaga and Bruno Mars’s Grammy-winning ballad, “Die With a Smile,” the Glee alum opened up about his personal connection to the dance genre—and why he suddenly felt a wave of emotion during rehearsals.

“I was born in Costa Rica, so like people were dancing salsa, merengue, bachata, but I was always an observer. So I’m really excited to tap into my roots,” Harry said of his heritage during the intro package that played before his performance.

After he and Jenna went through their routine in the rehearsal studio, he started tearing up.

“I don’t think I’ve ever felt such a strong emotion damn after dancing. I didn’t realize it, but Salsa is very meaningful to me,” Harry admitted. “I’ve kind of avoided doing salsa dancing for so long. I associated it with the struggles that my family went through, and I went through, moving from Costa Rica to America. But I just got a wave of emotion of just being really sad that I’ve missed the joy of salsa dancing for so long.”

While DWTS fans generally empathized with Harry’s feelings surrounding the routine, others joked about the lack of context in how his package was edited and claimed his reaction was “performative.”

“There’s something very performative about him,” one person wrote, to which another responded, “No he’s very open and honest and kind. I love him.”

A viewer on Reddit separately came to Harry’s defense, writing, “Its always so sad to see someone feeling disconnected from their culture AND it should be cool for people to possibly connect with that and anyone hating has never experienced that emotion most likely.”

Another person who quietly weighed in on the rehearsal package? That’d be Bachelor Nation’s Tyler Cameron, who was eliminated from the competition on October 27.

Fans raised eyebrows when Tyler reposted a TikTok poking fun at Harry’s tearful experience. “Harry Shum jr when the chipotle worker asks if he wants salsa on his burrito bowl,” the post reads, over a video of American Horror Story star Jessica Lange crying.

Once DWTS devotees caught wind of Tyler’s repost, they couldn’t tell if the joke went too far.

“So strange considering Tyler’s still in the comments on literally everyone’s posts every week cheering them on :(,” one person wrote, while another called out the eliminated dancer, writing, “So distasteful for Tyler to repost.”

Another person shared, “i was liking tyler but he still is talking about getting voted off weeks later😭 he’s being a weirdo right now.”

As of right now, the video is still filed under Tyler’s TikTok reposts. He has yet to speak on it, if at all, but he did share his thoughts on Jenna Dewan and Tatyana Ali’s performances on his Instagram Stories posted on November 11.

Harry also hasn’t publicly responded to the online chatter surrounding his performance, but I’ll be keeping an eye out to see if anything else unfolds!

#6783. Rosie Huntington-Whiteley and Wardrobe.NYC Feted Their Next Collaboration at Chateau Marmont

Los Angeles may slow down on Sunday evenings, but this past weekend, Chateau Marmont was anything but quiet. On the patio of the hotel’s penthouse, Wardrobe.NYC hosted an al fresco dinner celebrating its latest collaboration with supermodel Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, who also serves as fashion director at FWRD, where the 15-piece collection is exclusively available to shop.

Despite the gusty weather—which left half the table in near darkness as candles repeatedly blew out—longtime fans of the brand turned out for the occasion, including Laura Harrier, Lauren Perez, Vittoria Ceretti, Kirsty Godso, and Stephanie Suganami.

Not only was the first installment of the ongoing collaboration—a lineup of sculptural black-and-white separates that launched in 2024—a smashing success, but it also sparked a close bond between the brand and the style maven. “After the first one, we didn’t want it to be over,” Christine Centenera, a stylist and Vogue Australia’s editor-in-chief who co-founded the line with designer Josh Goot, told Vogue. Centenera was similarly effusive about Huntington-Whiteley’s magnetic energy and work ethic. “She’s really funny and can take criticism well, and she always has a creative solution,” Centenera noted.

The team took cues from the first drop when designing the second installment. “One of the top-selling pieces from the original line was a sculpted blazer, and so that kind of architectural feel was brought through into this collection,” Huntington-Whiteley, in a shearling stole from the capsule, explained. She then turned her attention to Ceretti, who was hobnobbing near the bar. “Vittoria is wearing the really cool shrunken leather bomber and great leather trousers [from the collection],” she added.

Huntington-Whiteley was equally grateful for the trio’s growing friendship. “I get to spend time with two incredibly talented people in the industry, two of my close friends, and create my dream collection that I want to fill my own wardrobe with,” she said. Ultimately, Huntington-Whiteley wanted to develop a versatile lineup that she could mix and match during her hectic international schedule. “It was all about creating a collection that I can keep in my suitcase and travel around with,” she shared. “It’s about the idea of a little black dress and those staple pieces that can take you from trips to events.”

For the first hour of the evening, guests mingled over cocktails and wine, taking in the twinkling city skyline. “The view here is magic,” said entrepreneur Perez, who arrived in a cropped blazer and slouchy low-rise trousers from the label.

Meanwhile, Raissa Gerona, Revolve’s president of brand and partnerships, looked around the lively crowd, appreciative of the community that had come together for the occasion. “Everybody’s so busy, people have kids, and it’s a Sunday, so people have work tomorrow,” she said. “So for everyone to show up to support Rosie and the brand, that’s the best thing I can ask for.”

Attendees settled into their seats at a candlelit table on the terrace, where servers promptly arrived with baguettes and French butter, roasted beet salad with goat cheese, burrata and tomatoes, and yellowtail crudo. Centenera and Huntington-Whiteley then rose to address the intimate crowd, thanking everyone—as much as possible over the whistling wind—for coming out to celebrate their latest creative pursuit.

Once guests finished their first round of dishes, the main course arrived: roasted chicken with mixed vegetables, and prime petit filet with asparagus spears, grilled broccolini, and truffle fries. The night ended on a sweet note, with bowls of berries and cream served for dessert, banishing any sign of the Sunday scaries.

Rosie Huntington-Whiteley, Vittoria Ceretti

#6778. How to Plan a Trip to Easter Island, the Filming Location for ‘Wild Horse Nine’

After making a splash during the film festival circuit, director Martin McDonagh’s Wild Horse Nine has finally hit theaters. The dark comedy-thriller starring John Malkovich and Sam Rockwell follows two CIA operatives on their 1973 mission to the remote Pacific outpost of Easter Island. Followers of McDonagh’s work are no stranger to his interest in mind-bending landscapes (who can forget the melancholic ocean cliffs in The Banshees of Inisherin?). But the decision to shoot on Easter Island—or Rapa Nui, its indigenous name—was especially history-making.

To begin, Wild Horse Nine is not only the largest Hollywood production ever shot on Rapa Nui, but it’s also only the second. Then, of course, there are the herculean logistics of shipping period sets by boat to an island that sits more than 2,200 miles west of South America. And most critically, the crew worked hand-in-hand with local officials to coordinate filming locations that would not disrupt the archaeological sites or the sacred Rapa Nui culture. Notably, almost half of the island is part of the protected Rapa Nui National Park.

As British Vogue’s film and culture editor Radhika Seth notes, it’s a story that wins you over emotionally, and that ultimately stays with you. And if you come away from the theater with a burning desire to journey to Easter Island, let the expert-led guide below inform your 2027 travel plans.

Easter Island, or Rapa Nui, was the filming location for Martin McDonagh’s Wild Horse Nine. The island sits more than 2,200 miles west of South America and is among the most remote inhabited places in the world. It is famous for its nearly 1,000 moai statues scattered across the landscape.

Rapa Nui is an island in the eastern Pacific Ocean long regarded as one of the most remote inhabited places on Earth. It is located 2,200 miles west of mainland Chile and is the easternmost outpost within the chain of Polynesian islands.

“I think there’s something incredibly special about traveling somewhere that still requires a little more effort to reach,” Carola Fresno, Timbuktu Travel’s Latin America specialist, says. Arriving by air requires a five-hour flight from Santiago, Chile, and flight connections are often limited. Because of the long-haul journey, Fresno recommends a minimum of three nights on Rapa Nui, but suggests four or five if you can swing it. “There’s so much more to the island than the famous moai statues, and it’s worth leaving time to explore properly and get under the skin of the island and the culture,” she adds.

For an oceanside stay that’s within walking distance of the island’s main town, check into Nayara Hangaroa. Each of its 75 guest rooms comes with a private terrace, and the design is driven by natural materials like cypress logs, clay, and volcanic stone. Sustainability programs go beyond the expected solar panels, extending to a local initiative that collects, recycles, and compresses and molds microplastics to transform waste material into moai figurines—a clever keepsake that reminds visitors of the importance of protecting the surrounding ocean. Guest experiences are shaped by the Hitorangi family, part-owners and cultural stewards of Nayara Hangaroa, and range from traditional dance classes and cooking demonstrations, to visits to a local beekeeper to learn why the honeybees of Rapa Nui are thought to be the world’s healthiest.

If your nationality allows you to enter Chile without a visa, one is not required for Rapa Nui. However, visitors are mandated to complete an online entry form.

It’s possible for visitors to rent a car, scooter, or bicycle to explore Rapa Nui, but many of the archaeological sites are regulated and require an accredited local guide. “Personally, I think the real value of visiting Rapa Nui lies in understanding its culture, and that’s where a knowledgeable local guide becomes invaluable,” Fresno says, who always recommends that her travelers explore with a driver and local guide.

Luxury tour operator Abercrombie & Kent partnered with Searchlight Pictures to create a Wild Horse Nine itinerary inspired by the film. “We’re seeing growing interest in film-inspired travel, particularly when a destination plays such an important role in the story,” explains A&K’s Adam Fogg, who created the itinerary. The journey begins in Santiago, discovering the events surrounding the 1973 coup highlighted in the film, eventually transporting travelers to the remote shores of Rapa Nui to explore its ancient culture and archaeology. On the island, guests enjoy dinner in a local family’s home before embarking on a nocturnal exploration. “Inspired by the legends of Haumaka and Hotu Matu'a, they discover how early voyagers crossed vast stretches of the Pacific using the stars, ocean currents, and natural elements to guide them,” Fogg says.

Beyond the film, excursions on the island range from trekking volcanic craters to horse riding up to the island’s highest point on Mount Terevaka. “One of my absolute favorite experiences is hiking along the northern coast of the island,” Fresno says. This hike showcases a quieter side of Rapa Nui and finishes at the white sands of Anakena Beach. For travelers eager to visit one of the island’s dormant volcanoes, Fresno points to a longer hike in the Poike area, which explores the volcanic landscapes and caves before continuing towards Ahu Tongariki, where 15 moai stand on a single ceremonial platform. “It’s one of the most mesmerizing sites on the island.”

If you’re a diver, the island’s volcanic origins can be explored via underwater cliffs, caves, and rock formations. “The water is this extraordinary, almost electric shade of blue,” Fresno says. “And while you won’t necessarily find the abundance of coral and marine life associated with some tropical diving destinations, the combination of exceptional visibility, volcanic scenery and the vastness of the surrounding Pacific makes diving here really special.”

Fogg notes that one of the most impactful ways to support the local community is being thoughtful about where your tourism dollars go, which includes dining at family-run restaurants. And while Rapa Nui isn’t a destination brimming with elaborate fine dining, that’s not really the point of coming here. Travelers will encounter plenty of Polynesian influences in the local cooking, as well as Chilean flavors. “I always encourage my travelers to try the seafood, particularly the tuna,” Fresno says. “The island’s isolation…is generally much more relaxed and informal, and I think that’s part of its charm.” While here, seek out an umu, which is a traditional Polynesian method of cooking food in an earth oven using heated stones.

In addition to dining at family-run restaurants and booking family-owned hotels, respecting archaeological sites and local traditions is equally important. “The island's moai and ceremonial sites hold profound spiritual and cultural significance, and visiting responsibly helps protect this extraordinary heritage,” he says.

Rapa Nui has a subtropical climate with rain throughout the year. The warmest month is February, with highs around 80 to 82 degrees Fahrenheit, while the coldest tends to be August, with lows around 59 to 63. The best time of year to visit Easter Island is between April and May and September through October when the temperatures are mild, and there are fewer tourists. If your priority is to have ideal beach conditions, consider January, February, or March (but expect bigger crowds).

#6775. These Long-Lasting Tubing Mascaras Survive Sweat, Tears, and Long Days

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

I’ve always been particular about mascara. I need a formula that lengthens my lashes to the heavens, won’t flake or smudge, and stays locked in place from morning to night. After years of testing and reviewing mascaras as a beauty editor, I’ve found that there is only one type of formula that manages to do it all: tubing mascaras.

The best tubing mascaras are in a league of their own. Instead of simply coating lashes with pigment, they wrap each individual lash in tiny, water-resistant polymers that form a flexible “tube” around the hair. The result? A mascara that won’t smear, flake, or run throughout the day, yet slides off effortlessly with warm water when it’s time to take it off.

It almost sounds too good to be true, right? The best tubing mascaras do feel like magic—team Cosmo would know. We’ve tried every tubing mascara out there, and somehow we narrowed our favorites down to this list of seven foolproof (and smudge-proof) formulas, below.

Why we love it: Ask any beauty editor or makeup artist what their favorite tubing mascara is, and most will mention this magic formula. “Liquid Lash Extensions is the tried-and-true, the pioneer of tubing mascaras,” says professional makeup artist Brittany Whitfield. “It’s my absolute favorite for length.” This takes natural lashes to new heights and won’t smudge—even in high heat—yet it still easily washes off at the end of the day. Plus, the formula comes in eight hues, ranging from black to ivy green for plenty of makeup experimentation.

One Cosmo editor says: “I’m a bit of a mascara snob, and I always find myself coming back to this formula. This is completely flake and smudge-free and keeps my lashes looking full and long until I wash my makeup off at the end of the night. It’s a top three mascara of all time in my book.” — MARY HONKUS, CONTRIBUTING BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: For those who want more natural-looking fringe that still appears fresh after 12+ hours, Merit’s clean formula is ultra-reliable. This imparts a soft black tint and ever so slightly lengthens lashes to achieve the no-makeup makeup look in just a few swipes.

One Cosmo editor says: “Clean Volume is the perfect name for this surprisingly un-clumpy volumizing mascara. The formula coats every lash, making my eyelashes look thicker and more fanned out, but it doesn't give them a spidery or clumped together look at all. The wand really helps separate every lash, and it even helps hold a curl for quite a few hours.”—BETH GILLETTE, BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Don’t let the mini wand on this mascara deceive you—this mascara packs a lot of punch into just a few swipes, and helps you get into all the crevices of your eye to coat every last lash. And it easily comes off with a cleansing balm or some micellar water, without pulling out any of your precious hairs.

One Cosmo editor says: “This tiny mascara should be considered sorcery first, a beauty product second. Upon first impression, I saw the short, stubby spoolie and couldn’t fathom liking the applicator. In just about five swipes, it completely lifted my lashes (no curler in sight) and lengthened them beautifully. It clings to and elongates tiny hairs I didn’t even know existed, completely opening up my eyes in the process.”—JASMINE HYMAN, ASSISTANT BEAUTY EDITOR

Why we love it: Clean mascaras often claim to be flake-free, but this one from Caliray is one of the only formulas that actually delivers. This creates a gorgeous falsie effect in just a few swipes, and stays locked in place. Not even high humidity can make this budge. Come hell or high water, it truly has your back.

One Cosmo editor says: "I love the idea of tubing mascara, but often get annoyed by the flakes throughout the day. But Caliray's is above that, offering lots of length and lift without chunks of mascara falling on my face. And the dark-brown shade basically looks black on my lashes, just ever-so-slightly softer."—BG

Why we love it: “Are you wearing falsies?” You’ll probably get this question when using the Tartelette Tubing Mascara. This formula uses micro-tubing polymers to wrap every individual lash in a conditioning, volumizing, and lengthening blanket. Beyond making lashes look incredible, it nourishes them so they can grow long and strong, too.

One Cosmo expert says: “Tarte's tubing mascara is my default mascara for everyday wear. It doesn't smudge or give me raccoon eyes EVER. I use the black on my top lashes and brown on the bottom. And compared to other tubing mascaras it doesn't tube off mid-day and leave weird flecks under your eyes.”—SARAH MABERRY, COMMERCE EDITOR

Time and time again, we’re amazed by how drugstore mascaras outperform luxury formulas. As far as tubing mascaras go, this one from CoverGirl takes the cake. Its longevity is totally unmatched—it lasts for 30 hours, making it the perfect party girl mascara—and beautifully separates each lash. Every coat creates even more definition sans-clumps for a fanned out, eye-opening effect.

One Cosmo editor says: “The brush is perfection, applying pigment evenly and smoothly with zero clumps. Unlike many tubing mascaras that feel wet or sticky on the lashes, this one hits the sweet spot—just the right texture for easy coverage. I was equally impressed by the staying power. I sweat profusely when I Peloton and am constantly wiping my face with a towel during my workout. This mascara didn't budge—nor did it irritate my sensitive eyes."—LAUREN BALSAMO, BEAUTY DIRECTOR

Why we love it: If you want seriously thick fringe—I’m talking so voluminous your lashes could be mistaken for extensions—a lash primer is a necessity. Luckily, this mascara comes with one built-in. On one side, you’ll find a conditioning primer that nourishes and lengthens, while the other end volumizes and deposits inky black pigment. The end result? Drama for days.

One Cosmo editor says: “Out of all the mascaras I’ve tried lately, this gets me the most compliments. I love the brown primer—it truly helps prep my lashes for the mascara itself, and I love that it’s not white. My lashes definitely look much fuller and longer when I use them together.” —JAYNE LEMIRE, EXECUTIVE ASSISTANT

Not at all. In fact, tubing mascara is less likely to damage lashes than regular mascara because of how easily it washes off—all you need is a bit of water, and the tubing particles slide right off without any rubbing or soap.

Whitfield says that removing tubing mascaras feels like conducting Cinderella’s fairy godmother’s spell. “The second the clock strikes midnight, and you get in the shower or wash your face, the fibers literally crumble right off,” she says. “You’re not fighting against the current with waterproof pigment or oily eye makeup remover to get it off. The water rinses off the mascara with minimal effort and without damaging your lashes.”

Mary Honkus is a contributor for Cosmopolitan with over eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing beauty stories. She’s an authority in all beauty categories and is very particular about mascara. For this story, she tested dozens of tubing mascaras and consulted with other Cosmo editors and a makeup artist to find the best formulas that elongate lashes and stay put until you wash your face at the end of the night.

Jasmine Hyman is the Assistant Beauty Editor at Cosmopolitan, where she writes about the latest beauty trends and must-have products. Her most prized beauty possessions are a meticulous skincare routine and salon blowouts. You’ll also likely find her in bed reading a good book or endlessly scrolling TikTok (spoiler: it’s usually the latter) while listening to Harry Styles’ entire discography on repeat. Follow her on Insta to be inundated with pictures of her meals.

#6771. RIP to LIV, the Golf League No One Ever Really Cared About

The most remarkable thing about the era of LIV Golf, a five-year run characterized by near-parodic blunders and missteps, is that leadership and its public-facing talent don’t appear to have learned any lessons from their many failures.

From the beginning, the upstart league, established in 2021 as a rival to the dominant PGA Tour, was plagued by its own ineptitude. It’s only fitting—after LIV’s newfound lifeline, private investment firm BC Partners, reneged on a $300 million investment—that the brand’s figureheads are now celebrating the end by taking a victory lap. Tone-deaf doesn’t begin to describe it.

LIV CEO Scott O’Neil—who took over for original boss and Major winner Greg Norman in January of 2025 and was faced with finding new cash flow after Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund announced in April that it would no longer finance the operation—clearly exists in a different reality than our own. “Together, our team delivered world-class individual and team competitions across five continents, built a global business, secured pathways to The Open and U.S. Open… and built a league capable of attracting both elite and rising talent from around the world,” he said in a statement late last week. It’s unclear if the farewell tour will continue, though, an even newer report suggests that BC is looking to purchase a bankruptcy loan from LIV’s current owners. If this Hail Mary works, LIV’s future would look much different than the one O’Neil has promised.

Another way to look at it? One of LIV’s biggest accomplishments was that the league, despite its intentions to diversify the game and bring fun back into a sport that had come to personify privilege and exclusivity, inadvertently consolidated the PGA Tour’s power as a monopolistic behemoth now primed to solidify its status as the only league in town. They helped usher in a new era of leadership and investment for the Tour, including the introduction of CEO Brian Rolapp, who is by all accounts the most well-liked and accomplished boss it’s had in quite some time.

LIV also managed to tarnish the reputations of its biggest stars. Jon Rahm, who was vehemently opposed to the dog and pony show when it was announced, only to sheepishly take a bag of blood money so big his great grandchildren will never have to work a day in their lives, is now one of the game’s great “What If?” stories. Prior to the US Open at Brookline Country Club in June 2022, the defending champion was asked about his thoughts on the upstart league. “Money is great,” he said. But Rahm and his wife thought about it further. “Will our lifestyle change if I got $400 million? No, it will not change one bit.” Just over a year later, he defected to LIV.

Then there’s Phil Mickelson, who was poised to become a fixture of the American Ryder Cup team and widely considered a shoo-in to hold a cushy job as an analyst for golf’s biggest broadcasting events. Upon joining the league, he admitted in a conversation with Alan Shipnuck that Saudi Arabia was a terrible bedfellow. (Shipnuck and Mickelson have disagreed over whether the comments were on or off the record.) “They killed Khashoggi and have a horrible record on human rights. They execute people over there for being gay,” he explained. But there was a caveat: LIV was simply a “once-in-a-lifetime opportunity to reshape how the PGA Tour operates.” Miraculously, he got that part right.

Prize money on the PGA Tour is greater than ever, the Tour’s main events have recalibrated, and its season-ending championship series has been completely reimagined. Mickelson won’t be allowed within 100 miles of it.

The league’s biggest attraction, Bryson DeChambeau, is theoretically the worst-situated of the game’s many turncoats, though he’s probably more excited about growing his YouTube channel than he is about playing golf anyway. No one’s put their reputation on the line for this gaggle of buffoons quite like DeChambeau, who spoke proudly about how LIV was “growing the game” and “bringing team golf to the world.” This past weekend, he shared a statement on the dissolution of LIV. “I still believe in team golf,” he wrote. “It may look different in the future, but I still believe it’s essential to growing the game.” A loyal foot soldier, even from the grave.

It was always about the money. Nothing more, nothing less. This was a cash grab of epic proportions, and golfers not good enough to earn their keep on the PGA Tour took a life raft and briefly found themselves in possession of luxury superyachts. Then, low tide left them holding bags of cash and not much else. Was it worth it? For most of these golfers, probably. They were past their primes when LIV began; they’re still past the primes now, and just a helluva lot richer.

The league positioned itself as a disruptor, and disrupted so poorly that they’ve turned the PGA Tour into the strongest version of itself we’ve seen since Tiger Woods was at his peak. All that bloviating about the good of LIV Golf from its loudest talking heads is a bunch of nonsense.

It’s hard to tell if these statements are one last line of defense or more genuine delusion from LIV’s last advocates. They came in with the idea of sticking it to the PGA Tour, which at the time was facing bleak circumstances, bogged down in nonsense before redirecting its energy towards batting this gnat into extinction. Ultimately, LIV helped the PGA Tour save itself.

Now it’s gone for good, and five billion dollars later, we’re not gonna remember a damn thing about it. Not the players, not the leadership, and surely not all the ways they supposedly grew the game while merely growing their own bank accounts.

→ Baca artikel asli

#6770. Kate Middleton Opens Up About Her Health Journey in a Rare New Interview

Kate Middleton is sharing a rare insight into her healing journey and cancer recovery. In a new interview published on November 11 in Country Life magazine, whose latest issue was guest-edited by her husband, Prince William, the Princess of Wales spoke to writer Robert Macfarlane about nature, mental health, healing, and more.

“I was told in hospital that my recovery would not be linear,” Catherine said in the interview, which focused on the role of the natural world in the future queen’s life. “There will be good days and bad days, that it will take time. This is the same in nature. It shows us that periods of rest, reflection and renewal are equally as important as moments of growth.”

Catherine has been in remission since January 2025, and has used her royal platform to speak about the ways in which time in the great outdoors has helped her recover. For her 44th birthday in January 2026, she shared a poignant final installment of “Mother Nature,” her video series that explored the relationship between wild spaces, mental resilience, and physical healing. Recently, in October, she visited Dose of Nature, a charity that focuses on nature-based mental health care.

Catherine, Princess of Wales, arrives for a visit to The Chase Community Hospital on November 11, 2026.

On Wednesday, November 11, Catherine made an appearance at The Chase Community Hospital in Bordon, Hampshire, as part of her work with the Royal Foundation Centre for Early Childhood. She wore what looks to be a new suit by London it-girl label Bella Freud, pairing it with pops of red in various shades, including a cranberry turtleneck, a burgundy Smythson bag, burgundy Gianvito Rossi heels, and a remembrance poppy in observance of Armistice Day.

At the hospital, the princess met with doctors and health workers who use the Alarm Distress Baby Scale (ADBB), a training that helps identify early signs of social withdrawal in infants in order to support them. This is the latest step that Catherine has taken with her early childhood healthcare initiative, which she launched in 2021.

In her Country Life interview, Catherine also spoke about her interest in childhood development, including how it impacts her own parenting style as a mother to Prince George, Princess Charlotte, and Prince Louis. “We are often so busy we simply forget to pause, slow down and notice the detail in life. It is something I remind myself to do with my own children; to give them freedom to explore, rather than constantly telling them what to see or what to think,” she said.

Catherine, Princess of Wales, meets nine-month-old Ruby during a visit to The Chase Community Hospital on November 11, 2026 in Bordon, England.

Meghan only wore her Welsh gold wedding band on her left ring finger, forgoing her engagement ring.

Meghan has a history of wearing just her wedding band while on trips abroad. Still, the piece is nearly priceless itself due to its material.

“Very little Welsh gold was ever mined, and it is traditionally used to make the British royal family’s wedding bands,” Hera Fine Jewelry founder and CEO Lauren Boc previously told ELLE while discussing Kate Middleton’s wedding stack. “Between its natural rarity and historic connections, I would consider her Welsh gold wedding band to be close to invaluable.”

She’s a full-time mom and entrepreneur, and that alone takes up a lot of time and work.”

#6767. How Marty Supreme Costume Designer Miyako Bellizzi Really Shops

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

The consummate downtown girl joins Bazaar’s podcast to talk about collecting vintage skirt suits and Supreme hoodies

The Good Buy is a podcast from Harper’s Bazaar in which editors Leah Chernikoff and Lynette Nylander invite celebrities, designers, models, and tastemakers to talk shop: what they buy, where they got it, and why it matters. Come down the fashion rabbit hole and take a peek inside the closets and shopping carts of the world’s most stylish people. Learn how they use style to tell their stories.

From Uncut Gems to Marty Supreme, Oscar-nominated costume designer Miyako Bellizzi is responsible for dressing some of the most memorable films of the past decade. A longtime collaborator with the Safdie brothers—Joshua and Benjamin—she’s brought the filmmakers’ stories to life with detail-oriented styling that feels lived-in and real. Whether capturing the ostentatious wardrobe of a 2012 Diamond District hustler or crafting 1950s-accurate ensembles for over 3,000 background actors in the Timothée Chalamet–led ping-pong drama, she’s proven time and time again she has a knack for storytelling.

In her personal life, this expertise has translated into an eclectic closet full of vintage finds. Known for mixing high-end designer pieces with streetwear pulls and 1940s workwear, she dresses herself with as much intention as she does the characters you see on screen. Showing up to film this week’s installment of Harper’s Bazaar’s The Good Buy, she paired a fitted blazer from her collection of 1940s and ’50s skirt suits with shorts she made herself, Phoebe Philo flats, and sculptural silver statement earrings.

“I love costume design, I love fashion, I love film, I love it all,” she said. “I love people, so I think that’s why.”

In the episode, Bellizzi dives into everything from costuming the screen to shopping for herself. Read on for highlights and watch the show in full above.

“I’ve always loved vintage, I think my parents put me onto it. My grandma had all these clothes from New York from the ’50s and ’60s that she didn’t want, and my dad is a big vintage buyer. Even my Barbies were vintage Barbies. But I also just appreciate well-made pieces, I appreciate good fabrics, things that you can’t find today. As a designer, it’s fascinating to see how things were made back in different eras.

“I collect vintage 1940s and ’50s skirt suits. It’s always been my favorite era, which was why Marty Supreme was a dream film for me. I think I started collecting in high school—we used to have vintage fairs in San Francisco, where I’m from. I like the shape and silhouette. This is what Mugler referenced. I’m a historical person and I love the shapes and silhouettes of Mugler, and I have a lot of Mugler, but also I know where it comes from, so I like to go to the source.”

“I don’t personally buy a lot of contemporary brands for myself, other than Phoebe Philo and the Row. I [shop there for] very classic basics, because I find all the freaky weird things vintage.”

“When I was a kid, I was like 5’9” in the fifth grade. I was really tall. And we’d always have to shop in the boy’s section. When I first started, I was at Details Magazine. It was my first internship; that’s why I moved to New York. But I always felt out of place. I never felt like I fit in with the fashion girlies of that time. And so I remember enjoying menswear and learning about menswear and tailoring, and I found that was more inspiring to me than being in the women’s fashion closet.”

“I was at Vice and I was doing all the fashion stories, and there was a mass exodus and it was the group that left that asked if I would be interested in doing a film. I had never done one before, and I was like, ‘Sure, I’ll try.’ Then, [with the Safdie brothers,] we had mutual friends. The film industry is very small. Everything is timing. I never thought I wanted to be a costume designer; this was not the goal when I first moved to New York. But I love it because it’s this family—you travel with your crew and you’re doing these jobs together and you live together. You’re living and breathing this alternate reality. All you’re thinking about are these characters. It’s almost like how an actor has to get into character. You have to become the character you’re creating.”

“It was tough, honestly. You have to think about yourself being presented in that way. Traditionally, most costume designers aren’t fashion girlies. I was excited for it but it was also very stressful. I remember I was working in Paris at the time and I was staying with [designer] Gabe [Semeraro]. I was freaking out. I went to a few vintage archives there, being like, ‘At least I’m in Paris. I can find a dress.’ And I did find a really beautiful red Galliano dress that I loved that I was going to rent, but I was like, ‘I don’t know, it’s so expensive.’ One night, I was home with Gabe, and he was like, ‘Babe, I’m just gonna make one for you.’ It was one of my favorite dresses I’ve ever worn or seen.”

“Every time I wrap a film, it feels like an achievement. I remember I was doing a film in Hawaii, and when I was there, I loved that the women wore their wealth on their wrists. They stack solid gold bangles [on their wrists], and most of times they have names of people in their families [engraved]. And they’re the most beautiful things. I figured out where to find them—at a flea market—and I got one and had it engraved. Now I’ve turned it into a thing where I buy myself a piece of jewelry or something really nice after I wrap a film."

“I am very thoughtful about what I buy for myself. I never want to spend too much money on myself. But I have made a folder on my phone of pieces I regret not buying. We were in Paris and went to Alaïa and I wanted this pink set that I didn’t buy and I still think about it.”

“Now that we are approaching fall, I live in a long sleeve. That’s my Bay area [upbringing] coming out of me. I need to have a XXXL long-sleeve T-shirt, heavy cotton. That is my comfort layer. I bring one everywhere. That and a hoodie. Those are my two hometown pieces. And when I get obsessed with something, I find a lot of them. I have a lot of Supreme hoodies, although my new favorites are 6397, because they have fun colors.”

“I was thinking about what I would buy if I had all the money in the world. It’s a pair of cuffs—or, actually, anything from this jewelry designer Art Smith. It’s kind of modernist jewelry from a different era, like 50 years ago. So something like that. Sophie Buhai is kind of the modern-day equivalent. Pieces that could be in museums.”

“I’m going to be realistic—I did buy four pairs of shoes this week: Chanel flats [from Matthieu Blazy’s first collection] on the RealReal, a pair of [Michael Rider’s] Celine jazz shoes, two pairs of Bottega woven flats.”
